 4 
2022 Annual Report of the Insurance Industry, Ghana
Welcome
Reliance and Limitations
The information and data presented in this report were 
obtained from audited Financial Statements and financial 
returns submitted to the NIC by regulated entities, as 
required by Section 155 of the Insurance Act 2021 (Act 
1061). However, it is important to note that the publication 
of summarized financial data does not guarantee full 
compliance with all the requirements of the Insurance Act. 
The accuracy and content of the summarized information 
have not been independently verified or attested to. 
Furthermore, some companies were unable to submit their 
audited 2022 Financial Statements in a timely manner, so 
their unaudited fourth quarter statements have been used 
instead.
Table 1: Firms whose information are based on unaudited Financial Statements
Non-life Life

Millennium Insurance Company Limited Ghana Union Assurance Life Company Limited

Genuine Insurance Brokers

Titan
Willis Towers Watson Insurance Brokers
In an ever-changing world filled with uncertainties, the 
insurance industry remains steadfast in its quest to provide 
a safety net for individuals, businesses, and communities 
alike. This report of the insurance sector does not only 
highlight the sectors accomplishments, but also examines 
the challenges faced and the innovative initiatives of the 
Commission crafted to navigate a rapidly evolving sector.
The NIC does not only produce annual reports, but also 
publishes quarterly summarised data of the insurance 
industry on its website at www.nicgh.org. This initiative 
helps promote transparency and accessibility of 
information to stakeholders and the public.
Additionally, the NIC provides key insights on the insurance 
industry to the Financial Stability Council (FSC), which 
demonstrates a collaborative approach to ensuring the 
stability of the entire financial system. The FSC comprises 
regulators from the financial services sector, including 
insurance, banking, securities, and pensions.
We appreciate the opportunity to present the 2022 Annual Report of the National Insurance Commission (NIC) and the 
Insurance Market Data. The report offers an overview of the insurance sector and highlights of key activities of the NIC.

The cumulative average growth rate of the insurance sector over the last ten years is 25%.
Minimum Capital Requirement
Pursuant to a directive issued in 2019 to increase the 
Minimum Capital Requirements (MCR) of regulated 
entities, the implementation of the directive took effect 
from January 1, 2022. This was aimed at ensuring the 
stability, financial soundness, and solvency of insurers in 
order to improve the market’s underwriting capacity and 
competitiveness. 
As at the end of the year, 95% of the insurance market met 
the new MCR and companies in non-compliance were 
taken through various regulatory actions.

 6 
2022 Annual Report of the Insurance Industry, Ghana
Chairperson’s Report
Introduction
The primary object of the NIC is to promote a fair, safe, 
efficient and stable insurance market. The Commission 
is also mandated to support sustainable market 
development, secure the protection of customers, support 
and encourage financial inclusion, and promote insurance 
penetration. Our resolve is to be guided by Commission’s 
object in order to bring about sustainable growth of the 
sector, improve access to insurance, improve customer 
experience and ensure market resilience.
The Commission has, during this period, remained focused 
on creating the enabling environment for the transformation 
of the insurance market in Ghana. Policyholder protection 
is a cardinal element of our function and as a result, our 
market conduct rules are anchored on fair customer 
treatment across the insurance value chain.
The insurance market comprises 26 Non Life firms, 18 
Life insurance firms, 3 Reinsurance Firms, 119 Insurance 
Brokers, 2 Loss Adjusters and 5 Reinsurance Brokers. 
A total of 13 insurance intermediaries were licenced while 
one loss adjuster exited. 3 insurance companies exited 
the market (RegencyNem, Exceed Life and Heritage 
Insurance). 
In 2022, the economic environment of Ghana went 
through some challenges. In spite of the challenges, the 
insurance sector has remained resilient.
FINANCIAL SECTOR IN 2022
Ghana’s financial sector consist of the banking, the 
insurance, the pensions, and securities sectors. 
In the 2022 financial year, the Ghanaian economy was 
faced with fiscal and macro-economic challenges which 
was exacerbated by the decline in global trade largely 
because of the slow recovery in the immediate aftermath 
of the Covid-19 pandemic. The Russian-Ukraine war and 
its attendant geopolitical tensions created a range of 
fresh headwinds for the African continent. the US Dollar 
remained strong with stable interest rates, which made 
it extremely difficult for Government of Ghana to service 
its debts due to rising inflation and interest rates. This 
situation was exacerbated by the downgrades of Ghana’s 
credit rating by international rating agencies in the course 
of 2022. Investors thereafter reacted to the uneasiness 
and considerable uncertainties arising from Ghana’s 
downgrade. The ultimate effect was intense pressure on 
the cedi, leading to its fast depreciation over its counterpart 
currencies. In the latter part of 2022, Ghana’s total 
public debt pitched at 94.3% of GDP and had reached 
unsustainable levels. Government in an attempt to rescue 
the situation opted for an IMF program and therefore a 
domestic debt exchange program was announced in 
December, 2022.
Financial Stability Council (FSC)
The Commission, as a member of the Financial Stability 
Council (FSC), worked with the various sector regulators in 
the course of 2022. This was primarily aimed at assessing 
the developments in Ghana’s financial system and 
the broader macro-economy, with a focus on policies 
implemented to mitigate emerging risks to financial 
stability in Ghana. The FSC also develops common 
supervisory approaches and standards for financial 
sector regulations. The NIC participated in the technical 
and working committees at the FSC, in which a number 
of common frameworks for effective regulation of the 
financial sector, including the framework for Supervision 
of financial conglomerates were developed. To enhance 
the operational resilience in Ghana’s financial sector, the 
Business Continuity Plans (BCPs) of the financial sector 
regulators were reviewed by the FSC.
Domestic Debt Exchange Program (DDEP)
Government announcement and further action on the 
DDEP was an invitation to debt holders to voluntarily 
accept to exchange their previous bonds and notes for 
repackaged bonds under new terms and conditions. Key 
elements of the terms and conditions included lower 
interest rates and increase tenure for the bonds. Primarily, 
the Debt Exchange involved a total of GHC 137.2 billion of 
domestic notes and bonds. The insurance sector exposure 
stood at 2%. Government DDEP policy was mitigated by 
excluding all Treasury Bills in totality, and notes and bonds 
held by individuals or natural persons. The DDEP created 
some challenges for bondholders with short term effects 
on disposable income. However, the medium to long term 
prospects for Ghana continued to remain positive despite 
the considerable uncertainties in the economy. 
The Central Bank provided forbearance to the banking 
industry to reduce their minimum capital adequacy ratio 
from 13% to 10%. The remaining financial sectors, including 
the Insurance industry and the Securities market also 
reacted to the announcement after they undertook various 
scenario and stress tests on the impact of the DDEP on 
their respective sectors. The effect of this reaction was 
the introduction of various regulatory forbearances by the 
various sector regulators in order to mitigate the risks to 
financial stability. 
The Commission introduced a moratorium on the 
enforcement of the minimum capital requirement (GHS 
50m) and the capital adequacy ratio (150%) effective 1st 
January, 2023.

NATIONAL INSURANCE COMMISSION
NIC Chairperson’s Remarks
The insurance industry continued on the path of growth 
in terms of performance and the bottom line. The table 
below highlights the growth of the sector from 2021 to 
2022 in terms assets.
GROWTH AND PERFORMANCE OF THE INSURANCE INDUSTRY IN 2022
Premiums
Total gross industry premium, including Ghana Oil and 
Gas Insurance Pool (GOGIP) premium increased from 
GHS 5.2 bn in 2021 to GHS 6.56 bn in 2022 reflecting a 
26% growth as compared to (2021) of 10.14%. The Non-Life 
sector accounted for GHS 3.51bn (53.4%) and life sector 
accounted for GHS 46.4bn (46.49%) respectively. 
The sector continued with its positive growth. One notable 
development in the insurance sector is the diversification 
of product offerings. Insurance companies in Ghana now 
provide a wide range of new policies to meet diverse 
needs of individuals and businesses, including annuities, 
microinsurance and repackaged products with enhanced 
features. Such diverse options seek to contribute to 
enhancing the protection of individuals and protections.
Insurance Penetration
Insurance penetration is a measure of the annual Gross 
Premium for the insurance sector compared to the Gross 
Domestic Product (GDP) current price. In 2022, the rate 
of insurance penetration stood at 1.1% from 1.14% in 2021. 
This reduction in insurance penetration was occasioned by 
the significant surge in inflation resulting in high variance 
between GDP current prices for 2021 and 2022. While the 
growth of premiums in 2022 and 2021 stood at 26% and 
10.14% respectively, the GDP current prices grew by 32% 
in 2022 compared to 18% in 2021. (Ref: Ghana Statistical 
Service)
KEY ACTIVITIES IN 2022
As guided by its strategy, the NIC embarked on a wide 
range of activities in the year across the full gamut of its 
supervisory works; spanning from prudential reviews, 
on-site reviews, public education and sensitization, 
enforcement on compulsory insurances, and other related 
supervisory policy and resolution responsibilities, which 
help to protect the insuring public. In addition to the 
strategy, the passing of the insurance Act 2021, (Act 1061) 
strengthened the Commission in the performance of its 
mandate.

 8 
2022 Annual Report of the Insurance Industry, Ghana
Chairperson’s Remarks
Regulations and Directives
To give impetus to Insurance Act 2021, (Act 1061), the NIC 
completed the development of several draft Regulations 
and Directives in the period. It is expected that these drafts 
will be passed in 2023 and 2024. 
Minimum Capital Requirement
Following the extension of the Commission’s deadline 
date from June 30th, 2021 to 2nd January, 2022 on 
the minimum capital requirement, almost all regulated 
entities met the new capital requirement. A total of 17 out 
of 19 life insurers and 26 out of 28 non-life insurers met the 
minimum requirements.
As a result, the market witnessed a merger of Phoenix 
Life and Ghana Union Assurance Life. Whiles Exceed Life 
Assurance, Heritage, and RegencyNem exited the market. 
Risk Based Supervision (RBS)
As part of the mandate to have a stable insurance market, 
the Commission has continued to work towards creating 
an environment that allows for continuous stability and 
sustainability of the industry. Over the years the NIC’s is 
shifting away from compliance based approach to a more 
risk focused approach to supervision. As a result, the 
Commission is strengthening its supervisory methodology 
by introducing risk based supervision (RBS)
In 2022, the Commission focused on capacity building of 
its relationship officers and designed an automated risk 
rating model, the Risk-Based Supervisory approach has 
been developed to allow for real time risk assessment of 
regulated entities.
IFRS 17
IFRS 17 is the new accounting standard to replace IFRS 4 
on insurance contract. IFRS 17 is expected to bring about 
enhanced transparency in reporting of insurers’ risks and 
financial positions. A roadmap was developed from 2020 
to 2023 by the Commission for the implementation of 
IFRS based on the Institute of Chartered Accountants 
(ICAG) requirement. Following the Ministry of Finance 
announcement of DDEP, a temporary halting of the 
IFRS 17 to understand the DDEP impact was made. The 
Commission engaged the ICAG on industry challenges in 
meeting the deadline.
Products
Annuities 
The NIC in 2022 announced the launch of the Annuity’s 
market. A total of five (5) annuity products were approved by 
the Commission and the regulatory framework on annuity 
was developed. Additionally, educational campaigns on 
Annuities were held in some selected regions across the 
country. 
Micro and Small Scale Products (GIZ Partnership)
The COVID 19 pandemic has remained an existential 
threat to the financial system and continued to linger 
in 2022, with its attendant challenges to businesses, 
especially micro and small scale enterprises. The NIC in an 
attempt to ease the effects of the pandemic targeted firms 
that sell micro insurance products to develop extended 
COVID 19 covers to low income and the under-served. The 
Commission in partnership with the Deutsche Gesellschaft 
für Internationale Zusammenarbeit GmbH (GIZ) provided 
subsidy to insurance firms that provide micro insurance 
products. In all, eight (8) regulated firms out of 16 firms 
(Insurers and Technical Service Providers) received support 
of 20,000.00 Euros each to provide extended COVID 19 
covers on their micro insurance policies. Public education 
was done by the Commission.
 
Regulatory and Compliance

 9 
NATIONAL INSURANCE COMMISSION
NIC
Environmental, Social and Governance (ESG) 
ESG risks and global response to the threats it poses 
have the potential to significantly impact the safety and 
soundness of regulated insurance firms, and the insurance 
sector more broadly. Climate risk, for example, is no longer 
as remote as it was in the past. As part of the collaboration 
with Financial Sector Deepening (FSD) Africa, a country 
assessment on ESG integration was completed in 2022. 
The result of the assessment led to the establishment of 
the ESG roadmap for the insurance sector in Ghana for 
implementation in 2023. 
Technology 
Following the passage of the Insurance Act, 2021 (Act 1061) 
the Commission is leveraging on the Act to provide a new 
layer of support for the development of the insurance 
sector using innovative technology initiatives. 
Motor Insurance Database (MID)
In the course of 2022, the NIC constituted an industry 
committee to review the progress on the MID post the 
launch. The outcome of the review was a number of 
proposed enhancements.
Marine and Aviation Insurance Database (MAID)
The engagement of the industry stakeholder sessions for 
the insurance industry was completed in 2022. Section 222 
requires the enforcement of commercial cargo insurance. 
To enforce this, the Commission as a member of the 
tripartite committee raised the issue of integrating the 
MAID with the Integrated Customs Management System 
(ICUMS) of the GRA. 
The Commission initiated negotiation with the Ghana 
Revenue Authority (GRA) and the Ghana’s Shippers 
Authority (GSA) on the integration of MAID with the ICUMS 
to ensure the enforcement of the compulsory commercial 
cargo insurance, pursuant to section 222 of the Insurance 
Act 2021, (Act 1061). 
Sandbox Regulatory 
With the entry of fintechs and insurtechs into the market, 
NIC created a sandbox environment which provides 
a limited space that regulators offer to insurtechs and 
innovators to test proposed innovative solutions on a 
limited scale before solutions go live to clients. To monitor, 
regulate and attract Fintechs and Insurtechs, the NIC also 
initiated the development of its directives on innovative 
licences. The sandbox directives are expected to be 
finalized and issued in 2023.
Cyber Security Authority (CSA)
The Commission sought guidance for the development 
of Cyber Security directives for the Insurance sector from 
CSA. Sections 35 and 36 of the Cyber Security Act 2020 
(Act 1038) mandates the CSA to designate entities as 
Critical Information Infrastructure (CIIs). The CSA in 2022 
designated NIC as a critical information infrastructure (CIIs) 
based largely on the information in the databases of the 
Commission. The CSA is also in the process of identifying 
insurance companies that may meet its criteria for CII.
STRATEGIC PLAN 2018 TO 2022
The end year for the 2018 to 2022 strategic plan ended 
in 2022. It is gratifying to note that we have collectively 
made great progress in accomplishing the key strategic 
imperatives that were outlined in the strategic plan. 
Amongst some of the key successes achieved are:
Strategic Objective 1
Increase Insurance Penetration - increase Gross Premium 
to GHC10bn
At the end of 2022 total premium is GHS 6.56bn. The 
growth was due to the key initiative of implementation of 
the Motor Insurance Database (MID) in 2020.
During the strategy period, the Marine Insurance protocol 
and its database development was completed. There 
is a requirement on the integration of the Commission’s 
database with the Tax Authority’s system to ensure full 
implementation of the compulsory cargo insurance. The 

 10 
2022 Annual Report of the Insurance Industry, Ghana
STRATEGIC PLAN 2023 TO 2027
The NIC’s Board initiated the development of the strategic 
plan for 2023 to 2027. The strategic plan is anchored on 
the following five key objectives:
1. Promulgation of the Regulations and Directives 
to the new Insurance Act, 2021 (Act 1061), and the 
implementation of compulsory insurances; 
2. Deployment of appropriate technology and 
innovation for growth and efficiency;
3. Ensure effective supervision of the insurance market. 
The effective use of Risk Based Supervision is one of 
the key areas of focus;
4. Facilitate the development of, and improve the 
inclusivity of the insurance market; 
5. Improve NIC’s capacity to deliver on its mandate. 
Agric Insurance policy has been completed and the fund 
to activate the products will be addressed in 2023 and 
beyond. 
Strategic Objective 2
Increase Insurance coverage from the current 29% to 40% 
by 2020 (insurance coverage means the number of lives 
insured at a given period)
Ghana’s insurance coverage at the end of 2022 is 44%. 
The key driver of this was the initiatives around inclusive 
insurance. The licensing of a number of technical services 
providers (TSPs) resulted in the sale of micro insurance 
products which increased the number lives insured. In 
addition, the Commission has initiated the development 
of a sandbox regime to allow for innovators and technology 
firms to test innovative solutions for market development. 
The results of these initiatives will be achieved post-2022.
Strategic Objectives 3
Enhance Public Confidence in Insurance
During the period, the Commission developed and 
rolled out the market conduct supervision framework. 
Additionally, the Commission developed the public 
perception index to measure and monitor the performance 
and confidence of the sector. The results of the perception 
index showed a public confidence rate improved from 
0.465 in 2019 to 0.6165 in 2022.
Strategic Objective 4
Strengthen Regulatory Bite
In the course of the period, the Commission led a 
successful implementation of the following: 
• passage of the new Insurance Act, 2021 (Act 1061);
• an upward adjustment of Minimum Capital 
Requirements from GHS 15m to GHS 50m and; 
• development of the risk based supervisory framework 
and systems and upgrading of the capabilities of the 
supervisory teams.
Strategic Objective 5
Improve Commission’s Effectiveness & Efficiency
In the course of the strategic plan period, the Commission 
worked on enhancing its efficiency through the following 
initiatives:
• Capacity building and training of staff on Risk Based 
Supervision to improve their competence. 
• Continuous professional development for staff is 
ongoing.
• Developed and rolled out a Performance Management 
Framework for staff to improve measurement of 
productivity.
• Implemented the required International Public Sector 
Accounting Standards (IPSAS) to ensure compliance.

 11 
NATIONAL INSURANCE COMMISSION
NIC
2023 OUTLOOK
Ghana’s insurance sector outlook remains bright. In spite 
of the global trade issues and the Covid-19 challenges, the 
sector has received steady and positive growth in the past 
years and this trend is expected to continue. Building on 
the foundation blocks of the 2018 to 2022 strategic plan, 
the Commission will continue on the path of creating 
the enabling environment for continuous growth and 
development of the insurance sector. 
Strategic Plan
The board looks forward to the approval and implementation of the strategic plan 2023 to 2027 strategic plan.
Risk Based Supervision
The NIC will continue to enhance its Risk-Based 
Supervision methodology (RBS) by undertaking risk 
assessment of regulated firms’ operations using the model 
in 2023 and beyond. The RBS approach will enable the 
NIC to identify and address weaknesses in governance 
and risk management processes across the industry using 
the nature, scale and complexity of regulated entities 
operations as the yardstick. The NIC will also continue to 
undertake onsite inspections and offsite reviews using a 
risk-based approach for the entire industry. This is expected 
to give sufficient comfort that entities are aware of the risks 
that they take and have in place sufficient safeguards and 
effective controls to mitigate those risks.
Technology
Government digitalization policy coupled with our 
strategic imperative to use technology and innovation to 
drive growth will continue to be a key focus of our plan. 
As a result, the NIC will continue to improve on its internal 
systems for enhanced data-driven decision making for 
the sector. New systems for fire insurance and reinsurance 
business will be rolled out alongside the complaint and 
claims management system to increase uptake on 
fire insurance, enhance and deepen local capacity for 
reinsurance business and lastly to improve customer 
experience respectively.
Human capital 
At the end of 2022 the total staff strength is 112. To improve 
NIC’s delivery on its mandate, the Board will commit from 
2023 and beyond to focus on the enhancement of its 
human capital to ensure required skills at all levels. The 
board will ensure that the Commission enhances and 
prioritizes its talent and its management which should 
lead to the development of appropriate succession for its 
sustainability.
ESG Integration
The Board will provide leadership on Environmental Social 
and Governance (ESG) and will champion the development 
of the enabling framework for industry conformance to 
ESG risk management, in order to ensure market resilience 
and sustainability.
Already, the Financial Sector Deepening (FSD) Africa is 
collaborating with the NIC in building capacity on ESG. 
The Commission will engage the board and CEOs of 
insurance firms on ESG in 2023 and also issue the 
regulatory framework on ESG.
IFRS 17
The Commission will continue its engagement with the 
ICAG for the industry on the deadline of the implementation 
of IFRS 17. 
The Agriculture policy and fund
The Commission will undertake stakeholder engagements 
in 2023 to design the modalities for the fund. 

 12 
2022 Annual Report of the Insurance Industry, Ghana
CONCLUSION
The Commission is committed to maintaining the stability 
of the insurance industry, protecting insurance consumers 
and promoting the development of the industry at large. 
Although the NIC is proud of the accomplishments 
outlined in this report, we will continue to improve on our 
mandate in order to reduce the huge risk protection gap 
and improve access to insurance. More particularly, we 
will also focus on emerging risks on the horizon (ESG and 
Cyber risks), which are far reaching on our risk patterns and 
very challenging to the insurance ecosystem. 
Notwithstanding the economic challenges, including the 
asset liability matching risks emanating from the DDEP 
program to the insurance sector at the end of 2022, 
the industry continues to remain resilient. The industry 
has made significant strides, contributing to the overall 
financial stability, risk management, and economic growth 
of the country. 
Through regulatory reforms, technological advancements, 
and increased awareness, insurance companies have 
expanded their product offerings, improved customer 
experiences, and played a crucial role in fostering a culture 
of risk management across the value chain. By addressing 
the remaining challenges and embracing opportunities, 
the Ghanaian insurance industry is well-positioned to 
further strengthen its role in the nation’s development.
APPRECIATION
I take this opportunity to appreciate the insights and 
guidance accorded by all past and present Board 
Members whose commitments have contributed towards 
the realisation of the Commission’s mission and vision. We 
could not have progressed so far without our great talents. 
The board is grateful with the diligence and hard work. 
On behalf of the Board, I wish to extend my appreciation 
to the Ministry of Finance, Insurance Industry and 
Associations, Policyholders, Other Financial Sector 
Regulators, Technical Partners (GIZ), and all other 
stakeholders for the continued support and confidence 
in the NIC. We commit to continue to work towards the 
maintenance of financial stability of our nation.
Board Chairperson
Ms. Abenaa Kessewaa Brown

of Management, a Bachelor of Arts degree from the University of Ghana, Legon, and a Diploma

Maastricht School of Management in the Netherlands.

 22 
2022 Annual Report of the Insurance Industry, Ghana
Circulars/Directives/Guidelines
As part of efforts to foster ongoing enhancements 
and progress in the Ghanaian insurance market, the 
Commission issued several circulars, directives, and 
guidelines in accordance with its responsibilities outlined 
in the Insurance Act. These were aimed to strengthen 
the equitable treatment of customers, safeguard 
policyholder interests, and promote adherence to sound 
market practices. The following were issued in the year 
2022:
1. LAUNCH OF INNOLAB INVITATION
In pursuit of enhancing insurance access and increasing 
insurance penetration, the NIC, in collaboration with 
Financial Sector Deepening Africa and GIZ Ghana, 
launched the ‘Innolab Insurtech Accelerator Program.’ 
This groundbreaking project was aimed to bring together 
various insurtech firms for mentoring, coaching, and 
fostering the development of innovative solutions to 
tackle the challenges faced by the insurance sector in 
Ghana.
2. NEW MINIMUM STATUTORY DEPOSIT REQUIREMENT FOR RE/INSURANCE ENTITIES
In relation to the recently introduced minimum statutory 
deposit requirement for (re)insurance entities, (re)insurers 
under the jurisdiction of the NIC were duly notified of 
the deadline for its implementation. Furthermore, they 
were apprised of the obligation to deposit 10% of the 
minimum capital requirement into their designated 
escrow account.
3. REMINDER - MINIMUM PREMIUM RATES
 In compliance with Section 256 of the Insurance Act 
2021 (Act 1061), the NIC issued a reminder to the industry 
regarding the authority of the Commission to establish 
minimum premium rates for insurance business. The 
industry was also reminded that non-compliance with 
this requirement may lead to the imposition of an 
administrative penalty by the Commission.
4. DIRECTORS AND PRINCIPAL OFFICERS IN ACTING CAPACITY
In adherence to the authority conferred upon it by Section 198(b) and Section 199(a) of the Insurance Act 2021 (Act 1061), 
the Commission established the following maximum durations for acting capacities, beyond which approvals will not be 
granted:
• Managing Directors serving in an acting capacity for more than six months.
• Vacancies in principal officer positions lasting longer than one month. 
The industry was also reminded that deviation from these specified timelines will incur penalties by the Commission.
5. MARKET CAPACITIES FOR 2022
Pursuant to section 2 of the reinsurance guidelines, the Commission informed the industry about the market capacities 
in respect of facultative businesses for 2022 as follows:
Class of Business Amount GHS(Million)
Fire 1,950
Engineering 1,200
Marine-Cargo 600
Marine- Hull 400
Bonds 280

 23 
NATIONAL INSURANCE COMMISSION
NIC
6. NOTIFICATION OF CHANGED DUE DATES FOR SUBMISSIONS OF PERIODIC FINANCIAL STATEMENTS 
AND RETURNS
Effective from Q4 2022, (re)insurers and (re)insurance 
intermediaries were instructed to observe the revised 
timeline for submitting quarterly financial reports, known 
as Supervision Department Returns (SDRs), within four 
weeks following the end of each quarter. Timely data 
sharing with relevant stakeholders was underscored by 
the NIC to uphold financial stability. 
7. NOTICE TO STOP CONTINUOUS VIOLATION OF TERMS OF CONSENT JUDGEMENT: 
Motor insurance companies were prohibited from 
providing insurance cover to high occupancy vehicles 
with one door and reported defects, as stated the 
Consent Judgment. The Consent Judgment involved 
multiple parties, including the Driver and Vehicle 
Licensing Authority (DVLA), Inspector General of Police 
(IGP), Attorney General’s Department, and others Strict 
compliance with the judgment was required to avoid 
violating the Road Traffic Act, 2004 (Act 683), the 
Road Traffic Regulations, 2012 (L. I. 2180), and potential 
contempt of court.
8. Conversion and Misuse of Privately Registered Vehicles: 
The Commission notified the industry regarding the 
DVLA’s concern about the unauthorized utilization of 
privately registered vehicles for commercial purposes, 
which is a violation of Regulation 19 of the Road Traffic 
Regulations, 2012 (LI 2180).
9. REMINDER ON REVISED GUIDELINES FOR THE OPERATION OF BRANCH AND AGENCY OFFICES 
Reminder on Revised Guidelines for the Operation 
of Branch and Agency Offices: Following inspections 
conducted by the Commission at the branches and 
agency offices of insurance companies, regulatory 
breaches were identified as a result of weak controls 
and inadequate oversight. In response, the Commission 
issued a reminder to the industry regarding the Revised 
Guidelines for the Operations of Branch and Agency 
Offices. Key points highlighted in the guidelines included 
the necessity of obtaining prior written approval from 
the Commission for the opening or closing of branches 
or agency offices. It also included the implementation 
of appropriate controls to mitigate fraud and financial 
losses, ensuring the safety of policyholders’ funds, and 
submitting regular reports to their respective head offices. 
Furthermore, the reminder emphasized the requirement 
for valid agency licenses for agents associated with 
agency offices.
10. Domestic Debt Exchange Programme (DDEP)
The Commission shared updates of Ghana’s DDEP with 
the Insurance Industry. It also provided the frequently 
asked questions (FAQs) issued by the Ministry of Finance 
and notified the extension of the program’s expiration 
date to December 30, 2022, as stated by the Ministry in 
a press release. These updates were aimed at providing 
clarity and keeping stakeholders informed about the 
program.

NATIONAL INSURANCE COMMISSION
NIC
It is worthy of note that there is consistent positive trajectory 
in the management and handling of complaints in recent 
years. In 2022, the complaints resolution rate reached 
an impressive 93%, showcasing a notable improvement 
from the 89% rate achieved in 2021. Despite an increase 
in the number of received complaints during this period, 
there has been a concurrent decrease in the number of 
pending complaints. This improvement can be attributed 
to the Commission’s unwavering commitment to adhering 
to established timelines for managing and addressing 
complaints.
The resolution meetings conducted by the NIC have proven 
highly effective, resulting in more favorable outcomes 
for complainants and a reduction in the recurrence of 
complaints. Additionally, streamlined processes have been 
implemented to enhance the efficiency of addressing noncomplex complaints.
The value of soliciting feedback from complainants cannot 
be overstated, and the Commission has successfully 
managed and supported this endeavour through the 
governance and directorate structures outlined in the 
Claims Management Guidelines.
Looking ahead, the Commission remains committed to 
engaging with and encouraging regulated firms to integrate 
robust complaints management mechanisms into their 
corporate structures. This proactive approach will ensure 
that complaints are promptly and effectively resolved, 
further enhancing the overall customer experience.
Resolutions of Complaints
In accordance with its mandate to safeguard the interests 
of policyholders, the NIC fulfills the role of an arbitrator 
to address complaints lodged by policyholders and 
the general public against regulated entities. The NIC 
aims at ensuring the resolution of these complaints in a 
manner that strives to satisfy all parties involved, thereby 
upholding the reputation of the Insurance Industry 
and enhancing public confidence. The following table 
presents a summary of complaints received across 
various sectors within the insurance industry.

2022 Annual Report of the Insurance Industry, Ghana
Motor Compensation Fund
The Motor Compensation Fund (MCF) has throughout 
its existence served as a crucial source of financial 
assistance for victims who have either been injured or 
lost their lives in motor accidents involving hit-andrun or uninsured vehicles. The MCF operates under the 
oversight of representatives from the Commission and 
Non-Life Insurers, who together form the Committee 
for MCF. In addition to compensating victims in these 
specific accident scenarios, the MCF also provides 
compensation to members of the public in cases where 
insurers have repudiated claims due to policy breaches 
such as change of ownership, inappropriate driver’s 
licence, or driving without licence.
The Awards Sub-Committee of the Motor Compensation 
Fund (MCF) conducted a total of 97 sessions during 
which 220 applicants were interviewed in comparison 
to the eighteen (18) sessions held in 2021 
The average claim per award regained its upward trend 
in 2022 after 2021’s rather sluggish performance. This 
was after the upward trend particularly between 2018 
and 2020. The table below provides a summary of the 
awards granted under the MCF.
Table 8: Summary of Awards from Motor Compensation Fund (MCF) – 2018 to 2022
2018 2019 2020 2021 2022
No. of awards 105 191 161 246 220
Total amount (GHS “m) 1.2 2.6 2.4 1.5 2.2

NIC
As required under section 223 of the Insurance Act 2021 
(Act 1061), the NIC established the Fire Control Fund to 
support state institutions in fighting fire. The following were 
activities undertaken in respect of the Fire Control Fund:
1. The Commission contributed Gh¢250,000 
as part of its annual support towards the 
construction of a Forensic Fire Laboratory for the 
Ghana National Fire Service (GNFS).
2. The fund also sponsored the Ghana National 
Fire Service’s maiden Fire Conference with 
GHS150,000. The conference was held in 
November 2022, under the theme “Enhancing 
Effective Fire Management for National 
Development”. 
3. The Commission, in collaboration with the 
Ghana Insurance College, organized a training on 
the Basic Principles of Insurance for 500 cadet 
officers of the GNFS in September 2022.
4. The fund continued its support with the 
sensitization and enforcement activities with 
regard to the compulsory fire insurance for 
commercial buildings and buildings under 
construction provisions under Sections 218 and 
219 of the Insurance Act 2021 (Act 1061).
5. In line with the above, the Regional Offices of the 
NIC embarked on a total of 1,063 compulsory fire 
enforcements for the year under review. 
6. The Commission continued its engagements 
with the Ministry of Local Government and Rural 
Development and other stakeholders for the 
incorporation of provisions of sections 218 and 
219 of the Insurance Act 2021 (Act 1061) in their 
regulatory licensing requirements.

Client Rescue Fund
The contribution to the Client Rescue Fund (CRF) 
experienced a notable 59% increase, reaching GHS7.3 
million in 2022 from GHS4.6 million in 2021. The CRF 
Committee’s effective strategies and the industry’s 
growth in Gross Premium as well as the high investment 
returns served as the primary driving forces behind the 
fund’s expansion. Under the Insurance Act, 2021 (Act 
1061), both Insurance and Reinsurance companies are 
now obligated to contribute to the Client Rescue Fund.
There were no market failures in 2022. Hence, no 
disbursements have been necessitated to fulfill 
policyholders’ liabilities.

NATIONAL INSURANCE COMMISSION
NIC Economic Review Report for 2022
The world economy continued to suffer from a series of 
destabilizing shocks. Just after two years of the COVID-19 
pandemic, the Russian Federation’s invasion of Ukraine 
and its global effects on commodity markets, supply 
chains, inflation, and financial conditions have steepened 
the slowdown in global growth. The Russian-Ukraine 
war, in particular led to soaring prices and volatility in 
energy markets. Also, a significant increase in agricultural 
commodity prices has exacerbated food security and 
extreme poverty in emerging market and developing 
economies. The slowdown in global economic activity is 
broad-based and sharper than expected, with inflation 
higher than seen in decades. Global growth was impacted 
adversely in 2022 with an estimated 3.2 percent growth 
as against 6.0 percent in 2021, making it the weakest 
growth profile since 2001, except for the global financial 
crisis and the acute phase of the COVID-19 pandemic. 
Global inflation also rose from 4.7 percent in 2021 to 8.8 
percent in 2022.
Sub-Saharan Africa
Sub-Saharan Africa’s recovery has been abruptly 
interrupted by the global turbulent economic challenges; 
high inflationary trends than seen in several decades, 
tightening financial conditions, supply bottlenecks and 
high energy cost. Rising food and energy prices are 
impacting the region’s most vulnerable, and public debt 
and inflation are at levels not seen in decades. Against 
this backdrop, and with limited options, many countries 
find themselves pushed closer to the edge. The Gross 
Domestic Product (GDP) of Sub-Saharan Africa slowed 
down in the light of the global economic turbulence with 
the GDP growth rate coming down to 3.6 percent in 2022 
from 4.7 percent in 2021. 
A shifting and tumultuous global environment is 
impacting and reshaping Sub-Saharan Africa’s outlook 
caused by the slowdown in advanced and emerging 
markets, tightening global financial conditions and 
volatile commodity prices. The rundown from the effects 
of the COVID-19 pandemic and the Russia-Ukraine war 
exacerbated the already fragile Sub-Saharan economies, 
pushing inflation levels high with double-digit inflation 
present in half of the countries thereby eroding household 
purchasing power.
The growth in the three largest economies in the region, 
Nigeria, South Africa and Angola experienced downturns 
with the exception of Angola. Angola’s economy grew by 
2.8 percent in 2022 as against 1.1 percent in 2021, Nigeria’s 
growth was 3.3 percent in 2022 compared to 3.6 percent 
in 2021 and South Africa from 4.9 percent in 2021 to 2.0 
percent in 2022. Angola’s economy continued to recover 
from the COVID-19 pandemic in 2022 supported by 
higher oil prices, improved oil production and resilient 
non-oil activity. The recovery in the crude oil price 
from $55 per barrel in January 2021 to more than $125 
per barrel in March 2022 as a result of the tensions 
between Russia and Ukraine shored up revenues and 
improved the medium-term growth prospects. Nonoil growth was broad-based despite external challenge. 
The Nigerian economy decelerated from a growth of 3.6 
percent in 2021 to 3.3 percent in 2022 due to persistent 
low production caused by low infrastructure and rising 
insecurity. South Africa’s economy in 2022 faced a series 
of global and local disruptions, including slowing global 
growth, geopolitical tensions, acute power challenges 
and inefficiencies in state-owned enterprises. Persistent 
power outages impacted negatively on the growth of the 
South African economy with two hundred (200) days of 
load shedding in the year 2022. The ramifications are low 
output and a fall in the growth of the economy from 4.9 
percent in 2021 to 2.0 percent in 2022. 
The indebtedness of the Sub-Saharan African region 
approached levels last seen in the early 2000s before the 
impact of the Heavily Indebted Poor Countries Initiative, 
though with a different composition. Public debt as a 
share of GDP is relatively high with Sub-Saharan Africa’s 
public debt to GDP ratio standing at 56 percent in 2022. 
Since the pandemic, the debt increase has been driven 
by widening fiscal deficits because of overlapping crises, 
slower growth, and exchange rate depreciation. The 
substitution of low-cost, long-term multilateral debt with 
higher-cost private funds has resulted in rising debtservice costs and higher rollover risks. Out of the thirtyfive (35) low-income countries, nineteen (19) are in debt 
distress or at high risk of distress.
Rising borrowing costs, muted growth, and elevated 
debt stocks have undermined debt dynamics for SubSaharan African countries, so that even stabilizing debt 
at an elevated level will require significant effort. Besides, 
the lack of fiscal space has also made it challenging for 
Sub-Saharan African countries to address the vast social 
needs, especially those in the most vulnerable segments 
of the population. Insufficient funding meant that the 
authorities struggled to scale up targeted support when 
the region faced record-high food, fuel and fertilizer prices 
in 2022. Poverty remained a major concern and looks to 
continue into the future with about 132 million people 
in the region estimated to be acutely food-insecure in 
2022. 
Most currencies in the region depreciated against the 
United States of America’s dollar in 2022. With regards 
to countries already grappling with high inflation, the 
weakening of their currencies relative to the dollar 
aggravated the situation, because the region is highly 
dependent on imports with a significant share of them 
invoiced in dollars. The currency depreciation also 
contributed to higher general public debt in the region 
because about 40 percent of the debt is external with 
an average of 10 percentage points increase by the end 
of 2022. 

 30 
2022 Annual Report of the Insurance Industry, Ghana
Economic Review Report for 2021
The Ghanaian Economy
The real GDP growth of the Ghanaian economic was 3.1 
percent in 2022 as against 5.1 percent during the same 
period in 2021. The non-oil GDP growth also dipped 
slightly to 4.3 percent in 2022 as against 4.7 percent in 
2021. The overall GDP growth was driven by the services, 
mining, quarrying and agricultural sectors. As at end of 
the third quarter, the real growth of the agricultural sector 
was 4.6 percent, the industrial sector grew by 0.9 percent, 
while the service sector growth was 3.9 percent. The spillover of the COVID-19 pandemic and the Russia-Ukraine 
war caused disruptions in production and supply chain 
and increased energy prices. 
The trade account recorded a surplus of USD2.75 billion 
in 2022 coming from increased export earnings relative 
to imports, compared with a surplus of USD1.10 billion in 
2021. Earnings from exports increased by 18.2 percent on 
a year-on-year basis to USD17.41 billion driven by crude 
oil, gold and other exports, including non-traditional 
exports. Exports revenues received from crude oil 
increased by 37.5 percent to USD5.43 billion as a result 
of higher crude oil prices. Also, gold exports receipts 
increased by 30.0 percent to USD6.6 billion driven by 
higher volumes of gold production coming largely from 
small-scale mining companies. On the other hand, 
exports from cocoa beans and allied products declined 
by 22.0 percent in 2022 to USD2.21 billion caused by low 
volumes of production. 
The import bill increased by 7.5 percent to USD14.65 
billion in 2022 due to higher payments for oil and 
gas imports. Imports of oil and gas, which are mainly 
refined petroleum products increased by 71.3 percent 
from USD2.7 billion in 2021 to USD4.7 billion in 2022. 
However, non-oil imports declined by 8.4 percent to 
USD10.0 billion in line with the slowdown in economic 
activities and sharp currency depreciation.
Ghana’s gross international reserves dwindled markedly 
by December 2022 standing at USD6.24 billion, 
equivalent of 2.7 months import cover. Comparatively, 
the gross international reserves stood at USD9.7 billion 
equivalent to 4.3 months cover at the close of December 
2021. 
Public Debt
The public debt of Ghana has hit the top level with the 
International Monetary Fund’s forecast of debt to GDP 
ratio for 2022 being 90.7 percent of GDP. However, by 
the close of November 2022 the debt to GDP ratio 
stood at 93.5 percent as against 76.6 percent at the 
close December 2021. The country’s total debt stock as 
at November 2022 was GHS575.7 billion with the share 
of domestic debt being GHS193.1 billion, that is 33.5 
percent while the external debt stood at GHS382.7 billion 
representing 66.5 percent. 
The increase in the public debt was also due to the 
depreciation of the Ghanaian Cedi to the US dollar and 
other major currencies as a higher percentage of debt is 
external. While the public debt was rising, external shocks 
at the global economy arena on the backdrop of the 
COVID-19 pandemic and Russia-Ukraine war impacted 
adversely on the revenue mobilization capacity of the 
country, resulting in challenges in the service of the public 
debt. The country’s debt service indicators and gross 
financing needs breached the sustainability threshold. 
Ghana remains a high-risk debt distress country under 
the International Monetary Fund (IMF) and the World 
Bank Group rankings after making it to the list in 2015. 
The high public debt and the inability of the country 
to meet its debt covenant, coupled with low revenue 
mobilization pushed the Ghanaian Government to 
seek a bailout from the International Monetary Fund. 
By December 2022, the Government reached a Staff 
Level Agreement (SLA) with the IMF. The SLA outlined 
measures that will put the economy on the path to 
fiscal consolidation. Some of the measures that were 
supposed to be included in the 2023 budget and also 
discussed at the SLA includes the enhancement of the 
revenue measures such as the increase in the Value 
Added Tax (VAT) rate by 2.5 percent, removal of the 
benchmark values on imports and the review of the 
E-levy to improve on the revenue mobilization. 
On the flip side, the SLA also touched on the austerity 
measures, which includes reducing the capping on 
transfers to earmarked funds from 25 percent to 17.5 
percent, reducing budgetary allocations to goods 
and services and the rationalization of executive 
compensation.
Another measure emanating from the SLA was the 
Domestic Debt Exchange Programme (DDEP) and the 
external debt restructuring. The Government embarked 
on the path of restructuring its domestic and external 
debts during the last quarter of 2022. Amidst resistance 
from the Trade Union groups, pension funds that are 
part of the domestic debt stock were exempted from the 
DDEP. 

 31 
NATIONAL INSURANCE COMMISSION
NIC
Economic Review Report for 2021
Inflation
Headline inflation for January 2022 was 13.9 percent 
compared to 12.6 percent in 2021 probably sending a 
signal of higher inflationary trend in the following months, 
with March 2022 inflation reaching 15.7 percent. The prices 
of goods and services increased in tandem with the high 
inflationary trend coupled with the depreciation of the 
Ghanaian Cedi due to heavy dependence on imported 
goods. By the close of June 2022, headline inflation 
stood at 29.8 percent as against 7.8 percent in the same 
period in 2021. In a bit to control the rising inflationary 
trend, the Monetary Policy Committee of the Bank of 
Ghana continuously reviewed the policy rate upwards. 
The rate which stood at 14.5 percent in December 2021 
was reviewed upwards to 19.0 percent in July 2022. 
Inflation for September 2022 increased to 37.2 percent 
compared to 10.6 percent in 2021. Again, the policy 
rate was adjusted upwards to 24.5 percent. Headline 
inflation closed the year December, 2022 at 54.1 percent 
compared to 12.6 percent in December, 2021, with the 
policy going up to 27.0 percent in November 2022. 
The high inflationary trend was due to increase in both 
food and non-food inflation in 2022 compared to that 
of 2021. Inflation on food items closed the year 2022 
at 59.7 percent compared to 12.8 percent in 2021, while 
non-food inflation increased to 49.9 percent at the end 
of December 2022 as against 12.5 percent during the 
same period in 2021. 
Interest Rates
Interest rates generally increased over the period in 
2022 in consonance with the upward adjustments of 
the policy rate by the Monetary Policy Committee (MPC) 
of the Bank of Ghana. The policy rate commenced the 
year 2022 at 14.5 percent in January and closed at 27.0 
percent in November 2022. In line with the increase 
in the policy rate, the interbank weighted average rate 
increased from 12.68 percent in 2021 to 25.51 percent in 
2022. Consequently, the average lending rates of banks 
increased to 35.58 percent in December 2022 as against 
20.04 percent in 2021. Treasury bill rates increased with 
the 91-day Treasury bill trading at an interest rate of 35.48 
percent in December 2022 compared to 12.49 percent 
in 2021. Similarly, the 182-day Treasury bill interest rate 
at December 2022 was 36.23 percent as against 13.19 
percent in 2021; while the rate for 364-day Treasury bill 
was 36.06 percent in December 2022 compared to 16.46 
percent in December 2021.
Interest rates on bonds saw a rise in the 2-year, 3-year, 
5-Year and 6-year bonds and closed the year 2022 at 
21.50 percent, 29.85 percent, 22.30 percent, and 21.75 
percent respectively compared to 19.75 percent, 19.00 
percent, 21.00 percent, and 18.80 percent respectively in 
2021.
Exchange Rates
The Ghanaian cedi depreciated against most of the 
major currencies during the fiscal year 2022. The cedi 
depreciated cumulatively in the interbank market at 
53.8 percent, 45.5 percent and 46.8 percent against the 
US dollar, the pound sterling and the euro respectively. 
Compared with the year 2021, the cedi appreciated by 
3.5 percent against the US dollar and, depreciated by 
4.1 percent and 3.1 percent against the pound sterling 
and the euro respectively. The cedi commenced the 
year 2022 at the interbank foreign exchange rate with 
USD1.00 to GHS6.0236, GBP 1.00 to GHS8.0882 and 
the EUR 1.00 to GHS6.7506. However, at the close of 
December 2022, the interbank exchange rates were 
USD1.00 to GHS8.5760, GBP1.00 to GHS10.3118 and 
EUR1.00 to GHS9.1457.
Developments on the Ghana Stock Exchange Market
The Ghanaian Stock Exchange Market (GSE) ended 
trading in 2022 with the GSE Composite Index, 
decreasing to 2,443.91 points compared to 2,789.34 
points in the same period in 2021, resulting in a year-onyear loss of 12.38 percent as against a gain of 43.66 in 
2021. The GSE-Financial Stocks Index (GSE-FSI) closed 
the year 2022 at 2,052.6 points recording a loss of 4.6 
percent compared to a gain of 20.7 percent in 2021. The 
year-on-year loss recorded was caused by uncertainty 
induced by inflation and exchange rate pressures as well 
as portfolio reversals. 
Market capitalization stood at GHS64.51 billion 
representing a growth of 0.02 percent at the end of 
December 2022 compared to GHS64.49 billion with 
a growth of 18.61 percent during the same period in 
2021. The marginal growth of 0.02 percent in market 
capitalization was as a result of the listing of Ashanti Gold 
Corporation and, the increase in the total outstanding 
shares of Anglogold Ashanti, Newgold Limited and 
Tullow Oil PLC.
The volume of trade in the Insurance sector increased to 
16,340,305 shares in 2022 compared to 3,112,769 shares 
in 2021. Consequently, the value traded also increased to 
GHS9,532,252.74 in 2022 as against GHS5,450,566.50 
in 2021. This could mainly be attributed to the recapitalization of the Insurance Industry. The Insurance 
Industry could take advantage of the Ghanaian bourse 
to raise capital to adequately meet operational cash flow 
requirements.

 32 
2022 Annual Report of the Insurance Industry, Ghana
Outlook for 2023
The IMF forecasts the global economy to grow at 2.7 
percent in 2023 as against 3.2 percent in 2022 amid 
pressures from the war in Ukraine, high energy and food 
prices. The IMF’s forecast indicates that a third of the 
world economy is likely to contract by 2023. The Fund 
also forecasts inflation to decline to 6.5 percent in 2023 
from 8.8 percent in 2022. The Sub-Saharan Africa region 
is projected to grow by 3.7 percent in 2023 because of 
muted investment and an overall worsening balance 
of trade in volume terms. Inflation will continue to be a 
challenge in the region with at least twenty out of the 
forty-five countries expected to run double-digit inflation 
in 2023. 
The projections of the IMF indicate that the Ghanaian 
economy is estimated to grow at 2.8 percent in 2023. The 
economy is expected to face inflationary trends in 2023, 
but will moderate towards the end of the first quarter into 
the second quarter. It is expected that energy and food 
prices will fall in 2023 on the backdrop of an amicable 
solution to the war in Ukraine. Also, the Ghanaian 
Government’s efforts to obtain a USD3.0 billion support 
from the IMF could slow down the depreciation of 
the Ghanaian cedi against the major currencies, and 
consequently lower the prices of goods and services.

Ghana Insurance Market Report for 2022
Introduction:
The implementation of the Domestic Debt Exchange 
Programme (DDEP) had a profound and far-reaching 
impact on Ghana’s financial sector, and the Insurance 
Industry was not exempted. However, despite its 
gargantuan impact on the insurance industry, the 
sector demonstrated unwavering resilience and 
continued to thrive.
Assets and Liabilities
The Assets and Liabilities of the Life Insurance, Non-Life 
Insurance and Reinsurance sectors are discussed in this 
section. 
Total Insurance Industry assets grew to GHS13.38 billion 
in 2022 from GHS10.97 billion in 2021. This represents 
a growth of 22%, a reasonable increase in the insurance 
industry’s assets given the financial and economic turmoil 
witnessed in 2022. However, this growth underperformed 
the 25% growth in 2021. The result of the increase of 
the MCR was expected to positively impact on further 
expansion of the insurance industry’s total assets in 2022. 
The impact of the DDEP has nonetheless affected the 
growth of the sector’s total assets.
Below are the details of the growth in assets experienced 
by the different insurance sectors in 2022:
• The assets of the Non-Life sector ended the year 2022 at GHS5.54 billion from GHS4.14 billion in 
2021 representing a growth of 34%.
• The assets of the Life sector grew by 15% from GHS5.81 billion in 2021 and ended the year 2022 at 
GHS6.69 billion.
• The assets of the Reinsurance sector grew by 17% from GHS1.02 billion in 2021 to GHS1.13 billion in 
2022.

newly acquired investment properties are properly vested 
in the insurance companies. This will ensure property
ownership risks are mitigated to the barest minimum.

Market Leaders – Underwriting Results
High management expenses continue to be the bane of 
the insurance industry. The Non-life sector was unable to 
maintain its profitability status chalked last year. With the 
exception of Motor insurance and Liability businesses 
that contributed positively to the underwriting results, 
the rest were not profitable for the year. Motor Insurance 
Database (MID) continues to support the underwriting of 
motor business since its implementation.
Market Leaders in the Insurance Industry1
Notable benefits continue to accrue as a result of 
competitive climate being witnessed among insurers 
in recent times. This is due to the gradual development 
of an enabling and robust insurance industry by the 
Commission with the full support of the insurance 
industry. The Commission continues to closely monitor 
measures put in place to prevent unhealthy market 
practices including premium rate undercutting and other 
unfair market practices in the industry to ensure that 
drawbacks to the progress of the industry are contained 
in good time. 
In recent times the NIC can report that the canker of 
premium undercutting and other unfair practices have 
reduced in the industry. The corroboration of this is the 
consistency in appreciable premium growth year in year 
out after the implementations of these measures. Also, 
to ensure insurance companies do not focus on merely 
topline premiums written, a more balanced set of criteria 
is used as determinants of market leaders. These criteria 
include:
1. Underwriting profits (for Non-Life insurers) 
2. Profit After Tax
3. Total Assets
4. Gross Written Premiums
5. Gross Claims/Benefits Paid

be sufficient to cover the total liabilities as of December 
31, 2022. Similarly, the total investments for regulated 
entities within a particular sector are sufficient to meet 
the technical provisions as of December 31, 2022, of that 
sector.

Reinsurance remains a vital risk management strategy 
utilized by insurers to transfer some of their risks to other 
(re)insurers purposefully to maintain financial stability 
by minimizing the likelihood of significant losses and 
catastrophic occurrences. 
Typically, the greater the variability in the severity of 
claims, the greater the employment of reinsurance in risk 
management. Hence, the Non-life sector employs more 
reinsurance relative to the Life sector. On average, the 
Non-Life sector ceded about 32% of premiums written 
to reinsurers in 2022. 
The graph below illustrates the percentage of premiums 
ceded to reinsurers by class of business.
Figure 27: Share of Non-Life Sector Total Premiums by Class of Business
Non–Life Sector
Even when all class of businesses reported losses in 
2022, the motor business made underwriting profit as 
a result of the implementation of the Motor Insurance 
Database (MID), which continues to be the game changer 
in the Non-Life sector Expectations remain high since 
the review of the MID has been completed, though the 
impact of the full implementation of recommendations 
is yet to kick in. Motor Premium grew from GHS1,165m 
in 2021 to GHS1,416m in 2022, representing a year-onyear growth of 22% and contributed 40% to the total 
premium of the sector. The Commission continues to 
rely on the collaboration of industry players to mitigate 
the canker of premium undercutting and significantly 
reduce fake motor insurance. 
Gross written premium for Fire, Theft and Property also 
increased to GHS850m in 2022 from GHS602m in 2021, 
representing a growth of 37%.
In 2022, insurance premiums from the Oil and Gas sector 
continued its growth trajectory with a growth of 17% 
though this represents an underperformance compared 
to 37% in 2021. This still reflects a positive growth when 
compared to the of -10% in 2020 and -24% in 2019. The 
premiums received were GHS414m in 2022, compared 
to GHS354m in 2021, GHS248m in 2020 and GHS267m 
in 2019. 
Liability and Engineering classes of business in 2022 
contributed about 6% each of Non-Life premiums 
at GHS224m and GHS195m, respectively. Marine 
& Aviation, Financial Guarantee/Bonds, Personal 
Accidents, and Other Short-Term Products contributed 
4%, 4%, 3%, and 1%, respectively.

Life Sector
Universal Life remains the dominant product in the Life 
sector generating 48% of the total premium to settle at 
GHS1,458m in 2022. Whole life and Endowment products 
followed with a contribution of GHS911m, representing 
30% of total Life premiums. In the third position, Group 
Life premiums stood at GHS219m, maintaining a 7% 
contribution to the basket of Life insurance premiums. 
The remaining products also maintained their share of 
15% to the basket with a contribution of GHS251m in 
2022.
Given that savings-linked products continue to dominate 
product sales, the need for the payment of claims remains 
crucial in contrast to pure risk products. Consequently, 
it is imperative for Life Insurers to consistently adhere 
to appropriate reserving and asset-liability matching 
practices, considering the inherent characteristics of the 
products they underwrite.

Insurance Intermediaries Market
Market Leaders – in terms of Total Assets
At the end of 2022, total assets held by insurance 
intermediaries amounted to GHS364 million, an 
increase from GHS270 million in 2021, representing a 
growth of 35%. Insurance brokers accounted for 88.8% 
of intermediary assets, contributing GHS323 million. 
Reinsurance brokers held 11% of the total assets, 
amounting to GHS40 million, whilst loss adjusters 
managed 0.2%, amounting to GHS0.9 million.
The diagrams below illustrate the distribution of sectoral 
assets as well as the asset values of the market leaders 
within the broking sector, reinsurance, and loss adjusters 
in 2022.
Microinsurance
Microinsurance continues to play a crucial role in 
reducing the protection gap and vulnerabilities faced 
by low-income individuals and the underserved. The 
NIC’s market development initiatives have resulted in 
the increase demand for microinsurance products. The 
introduction of the TSP licensing regime has continued 
to streamline and expand access to inclusive insurance. 
The Commission has introduced a number of interventions 
aimed at enhancing confidence in the inclusive insurance 
space. These include the implementation of the market 
conduct rules and facilitation of a number of TSPs to 
distribute micro and inclusive insurance products.
Some of the licensed TSPs are: aYo Intermediaries, Milvik 
Ghana Limited (BIMA), and MicroEnsure.

REINSURANCE & ANTI-MONEY LAUNDERING DEPARTMENT.
2022 Reinsurance Premium Transfer 
Insurers and reinsurers continue to rely on reinsurance 
as a key risk management strategy for managing the 
risks they underwrite. The underwriting capacity of these 
companies is essential in determining the amount of risk 
that may be retained on the local market. 
The Commission is enjoined by the Insurance Act, 2021 
(Act 1061) to approve all reinsurance arrangements. As 
a result, the Commission’s written authorization is a 
prerequisite for entering into a reinsurance contract, 
especially with a foreign reinsurer. This also applies to all 
overseas risk and premium transfers. 
The table below shows premium that was approved for 
overseas transfer in various currencies in 2022.

It is evident from the tables above that, overseas 
premiums have generally increased year-on-year. Even 
though there was a dip in Dollar transfers of about 
USD4.8million, a significant increase in Euro transfer 
in excess of EUR700,000 was recorded. Construction 
related risks contributed about 80% of the total Euro 
amount. 
The implementation of the new minimum capital 
requirement, which came into effect on January 1, 2022, 
has empowered insurers and reinsurers to enhance their 
retention capacities, enabling them to under write high 
value risks while also retaining a fair share. As a result, 
there has been a notable shift towards higher retention 
of risks within the local market.

ACTIVITIES OF ANTI-MONEY LAUNDERING AND COMBATING 
THE FINANCING OF TERRORISM 
The Commission continued to exercise its oversight 
towards monitoring and enforcement of Anti-Money 
Laundering and Combating the Financing of Terrorism 
(AML/CFT) compliance in the Insurance Sector for the 
year ending December 2022.
The Commission worked with the Anti-Money 
Laundering Reporting Officers (AMLROs) of the various 
regulated entities to ensure that deficiencies identified 
during offsite and onsite supervisory assessments were 
resolved.

INHERENT RISK ANALYSIS
The NIC continued to receive quarterly returns from Life 
companies. All Twenty (20) Life companies submitted 
data quarterly for the Commission’s analysis to 
determine their inherent risk level.
Analysis from the data received during the period 
indicated that the structural risk for most companies 
remained the same but business risks have generally 
improved due to an improvement in the AML/CFT 
regimes of the companies.
As at December 2022, only two (2) companies had an 
inherent risk of ‘High’. Twelve (12) companies were ranked 
‘Medium’ risk while six (6) were ranked ‘Low’. The two 
(2) companies whose composite risk rating was ranked 
‘High’ had their business risk largely driven by the volume 
of business. 

 56 
2022 Annual Report of the Insurance Industry, Ghana
REGULATORS FORUM
The Commission continued to play its role in fostering 
cooperation with other financial sector regulators. 
Members were enjoined to collaborate to ensure that 
regulated entities are not used to perpetrate moneylaundering and terrorism financing.
At its last meeting in September, 2022, members 
discussed progress made in addressing identified 
deficiencies during the face-to-face meeting with the 
International Cooperation Review Group (ICRG) - a setup by Financial Action Task Force (FATF).
Guidelines have been developed to enable regulated 
entities identify signs of any seeming ML/TF threats.
The NIC conducted onsite inspection activities at Life Insurance Companies as part of efforts towards ensuring 
effectiveness of their compliance regimes. 
Ten (10) out of the twenty (20) Life companies were visited for onsite inspections during the year.
 Some of the findings of the inspection exercise were:
a. Weak Board oversight;
b. Inability of some Boards to discuss AML/CFT reports in order to improve processes;
c. Inadequate reports that did not cover all the measures required;
d. Manual and unsatisfactory Monitoring and Reporting of AML/CFT compliance requirements;
e. Inadequate Know Your Customer (KYC) and Customer Due Diligence (CDD) measures; and
f. Lack of thorough independent assessment of AML/CFT measure.
Affected companies were continuously guided to resolve all outstanding issues.

NATIONAL INSURANCE COMMISSION
NIC
Research At A Glance
The integral role of the Research Unit is to conduct 
empirical and evidence-based research to complement 
the work of the Commission. The unit played an 
important role in gathering, analyzing, and interpreting 
data and information related to the Commission’s 
regulatory obligations. The following presents a summary 
of key researches conducted in 2022.
The Landscape of Microinsurance in Ghana 2020 
(Phase 2)
Based on the previous landscape research (qualitative) 
conducted, this study sought to update and improve the 
quantitative findings of the previous study. The report 
presented an assessment of microinsurance providers, and 
technical services providers. The study identified key issues 
facing the microinsurance industry and provided appropriate 
insights and recommendations for improvements.
Assessment of the Claim Ratio of Microinsurance Products 
sold through Mobile Network Operators (MNOs)
This study sought to identify the reasons for the low claim ratio 
of microinsurance (MI) products sold through MNOs. The claimmaking procedure laid down by the Commission to MI providers 
was assessed. This research helped the Commission to identify 
challenges associated with making a claim through MNOs. The study 
further provided recommendations that could be adopted for policy 
decisions.

 58 
2022 Annual Report of the Insurance Industry, Ghana
Assessment of the Motor Insurance Database 
(MID)
This assessment was conducted to evaluate the performance of 
the motor insurance database (MID) after its implementation in 
2020. The outcome of the research led to the implementation of 
recommendations to enhance the database.
Bi-Annual Journal (Insurance Hub)
The Commission has revived the publication of its biannual journal. 
The journal is intended to address matters arising in the insurance 
industry, give up-to-date information about the insurance industry, 
publish insurance-related articles and any relevant information that 
will improve the insurance industry, and inform new reforms and 
achievements in the industry. The 5th Edition of the journal has been 
distributed and currently, there are works on the next edition to be 
distributed.
Redefinition of Insurance penetration 
The Commission sought to present a better computation of 
insurance penetration in relation to other jurisdictions. The study 
argued on the need for the redefinition of insurance penetration 
rate. The study explained the reasons for the low insurance 
penetration rate of Ghana as compared to other jurisdictions 
within the same economic bracket. 
The study concluded that the formula for insurance penetration 
needs to be inclusive of health and pension premiums. This shows 
an improved and more realistic rate of insurance penetration in 
Ghana.

 59 
NATIONAL INSURANCE COMMISSION
NIC
The NIC as part of efforts to use innovation as the enabler 
for growth and transformation of the insurance sector, 
developed three major systems. These are the Marine/
Aviation Insurance Database (MAID), the Reinsurance 
Database, and the Complaints and Claims Management 
System. The following are highlights of the basis for 
developing these databases. 
Marine & Aviation Insurance Database
The Marine & Aviation Insurance Database is to serve as 
a central database that will hold information on marine 
& aviation insurance policies underwritten in Ghana. In 
addition, there will be a portal for insurers, agents, and 
brokers to update the central database with policies on 
marine and aviation. The system shall be integrated with 
the Ghana Revenue Authority’s Integrated Customs 
Management System to allow for the enforcement of 
the compulsory commercial cargo insurance pursuant to 
section 222 of the Insurance Act 2021 (Act 1061). 
Complaints and Claims Management System
A cardinal requirement in insurance regulation is 
adherence to Treating Customers Fairly (TCF). The NIC’s 
quest to effectively protect consumers and ensure that 
insurers meet their claims payment obligations means 
that the Claims and Complaint Unit of the NIC must be 
agile to handle complaints and settlement issues with 
speed and effectiveness. The aim is to build an endto-end centralized compliant handling system that will 
promote the expeditious handling of all complaints 
in order to ensure fair customer treatment across 
the insurance value chain in Ghana. The system is 
intended to enable the NIC interact with Insurers and 
Complainants in an effective and efficient manner in 
order to resolve complaints.
Reinsurance Management System
In fulfilment of Sections 72 and 73 of the Insurance 
Act 2021 (Act 1061), the NIC’s Reinsurance Department 
monitors reinsurance placements and premium 
transfers both locally and internationally. Over time, 
these series of data have become increasingly onerous to 
handle. To ensure efficiency in reinsurance placements 
and related activities, the Commission has introduced 
the reinsurance management system to automate the 
reinsurance process.
Special Investigations and Sanctions
In 2022, the NIC conducted investigations into 
underwriting infractions, specifically cases of premium 
undercutting by insurance companies, resulting in 
penalties being imposed. The Commission has also 
institutionalized a framework to authenticate motor 
compensation fund claims. 
In the year under review, the NIC in collaboration 
with the Financial Sector Deepening Africa (FSD) 
and the Deutsche Gesellschaft für Internationale 
Zusammenarbeit GmbH (GIZ) organized the Innolab 
Insurtech Accelerator Program. The program brought 
together twenty-five start-up tech 
firms across Africa with the aim to 
attract key players and disruptors, to 
develop ground-breaking innovative 
insurance solutions for the sector. The 
program was further aimed to create 
awareness of particular sections of the 
new Insurance Act 2021 (Act 1061) that 
support innovators, and as well as give 
insights on the draft sandbox directive 
to be issued by the NIC.
Ten Insurtechs were selected to 
undergo a three-month intensive 
program, that included, developing prototype solutions 
for the insurance market, receiving mentorship training, 
and building the capacities of the selected firms. A 
demo-day was organized to climax the three- month 
long program in March 2022, where the ten shortlisted 
firms pitched their solutions to players in the industry. 
In the end, three of the cohort (Figtech Limited, BeNew 
and Ibisa Network) were adjudged winners. They were 
presented with grant award of 20,000 euros each.
Innovation at a Glance

insurers’ total premium, including savings premium (i.e.,

four (4) percentage points to an average of 24% in 2022 
from 28% in 2021. GN Life, First Insurance, Metropolitan 
Life, and Vanguard Life experienced negative growth in 
2022.

Policyholder Coverage
This ratio compares a company’s equity and actuarial 
liabilities and indicates how much capital is available after 
all technical provisions have been considered. Generally, 
when the actuarial liabilities are more than 300% of the 
company’s equity, it is considered risky since the ability 
to absorb unexpected shocks may be impaired. GN Life, 
Metropolitan Life, Old Mutual Life, Prudential Life, and SIC 
Life had ratios above 300%. The industry average settled 
at155% in 2022 compared to 138% in 2021.

NATIONAL INSURANCE COMMISSION
NIC
Proportion of Investments
This asset quality ratio is calculated as a ratio of total 
investments (including cash and cash equivalents) to 
total assets. It seeks to establish the percentage of the 
company’s assets that are in investments as opposed to 
operational assets. Investment assets are generally kept to 
meet known liabilities, so a high investment assets ratio 
shows a positive position compared to a low investment 
assets ratio. The Life Insurance sector recorded an average 
of 86% for 2022. The higher the ratio, the better the 
quality of the company’s asset base. Only First Insurance 
Company had a proportion of investment to total assets 
less than 70%

Policyholder Benefits Cover
This is a measure of investment adequacy for an insurer’s 
liability. It is determined as a ratio of total investments to 
actuarial liabilities and seeks to assess the adequacy of the 
company’s investments to cover the policyholder liabilities. 
The Life sector recorded an average of 190% in 2022. A ratio 
less than 100% means that the company does not have 
enough investments to cover the policyholder liabilities. 
No company recorded a ratio below 100%. This ratio 
helps to analyse the adequacy of a company’s technical 
provisions. It must be considered alongside policyholder 
coverage ratio to ensure a company is not associated with 
the risk of under provisioning.

Retention Ratio
This ratio measures the proportion of the premiums not 
ceded to reinsurers. Retention among Life insurers in 
Ghana is generally high, given the nature of the life business. 
Quality Life and SIC Life had the highest retention ratio of 
about 99% in 2022. Generally, this high retention rate can 
be attributed to the high savings-oriented policies relative 
to risk businesses in the life insurance market.

Investment Yield
This ratio measures the return on a company’s investments 
(investment income) to total investment. It seeks to 
measure the quality of a company’s investment portfolio. 
The industry’s average decreased marginally from 8% in 
2021 to 7% in 2022. Three (3) companies recorded negative 
investment yields, while one-third of the life insurers had 
yields below 5%.

Return on Equity (ROE)
This ratio measures the return on Shareholders’ funds 
over a period. A negative ratio implies the company has 
experienced a loss, negative equity base, or both during 
the period. The outlook of overall returns was negative 
in 2022, recording an ROE of -5%. The industry average 
for the previous year was positive. Eleven (11) life insurers 
recorded losses after tax in 2022.

Return on Assets (ROA)
This is a ratio of profit after tax to total assets. It seeks to 
measure the efficiency with which management utilizes 
the insurer’s assets to generate returns for its various 
stakeholders. The averages over the past five (5) years 
have been relatively poor, however, the ROA increased by 
three percentage points in 2021, but could not consolidate 
these gains as it decreased by one percent to settle at -2% 
in 2022.

NATIONAL INSURANCE COMMISSION
NIC
Expense Ratio
This ratio calculates actual management expenses 
incurred as a percentage of total net inflows received 
from policyholders. It serves as a check on efficiency. The 
internationally acceptable threshold is a ratio of 40% or 
less. A high ratio indicates inefficiency on the part of the 
insurance company in managing its operations. It may 
also suggest most of the premiums received are going into 
expenses and not necessarily building up policyholders’ 
benefit reserves. First Insurance, GN Life, and Vanguard 
Life had expense ratios more than double the industry 
average. The average industry ratio in 2022 was 76%.
